class="prose-p">Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W. 14<em>th June</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I am much obliged for your letter of the 10th inst., informing me that “candidates for commissions in the British <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-509" data-page="509" data-folio="489" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n508_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 489">[p. 489]</button> Army must be of pure European descent, and are also required to be British born or naturalised British subjects.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I shall feel further obliged if you would kindly inform me by what Act of Parliament is this limit laid down for the candidates, to the exclusion of other British subjects of her Majesty of other descent and born in her Majesty's British dominions, such as British India and the colonies.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, Pall Mall, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">100/Candidates/1681.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">War Office, Pall Mall, S.W. <em>25th June, 1896.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—With reference to your further letter of the 14th instant, I am directed by the Secretary of State for War to acquaint you that the conditions for admission to the Army are not laid down by Act of Parliament but by regulation, and that the regulations are to the effect already conveyed to you.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Coleridge Grove</span>, Mil. Sec.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W. <em>26th June, 1896.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I am much obliged by your letter of 25th inst. (100/Candidates/1681) explaining that “the conditions for admission to the Army are not laid down by Act of Parliament but by regulation.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I shall feel much obliged by your informing me that if these conditions are not laid down by Act of Parliament then by what other authority are they laid down? May I also <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-510" data-page="510" data-folio="490" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n509_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 490">[p. 490]</button> request you kindly to supply me with a copy of such authority and of the regulations in which these conditions are specified ?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dababhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, Pall Mall, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">100/Candidates/1685. War Office, Pall Mall, S.W. <em>6th July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—With reference to your letter of the 26th ultimo, I am directed by the Secretary of State for War to transmit to you a copy of the Sandhurst Regulations, and also a copy of the Form of Particulars which is sent to all candidates who apply for examination for admission to the Royal Military College.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Coleridge Grove</span>, Mil. Sec.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Wandsworth, S.W. <em>8th July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have received your letter of 6th inst. (100/Candidates/1685) for which I thank you.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am sorry I did not make my meaning clear.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">You said in your letter of 25th ult. (100/Candidates/1681) “that the conditions for admission to the Army are not laid down by Act of Parliament, but by Regulation.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now what I desire to know is this. I have always understood that the only constitutional authority or power for laying down all such conditions is Parliament, while you say that these conditions are not laid down by an Act of Parliament. Then, what other constitutional authority has the power and has laid down these conditions according to which <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-511" data-page="511" data-folio="491" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n510_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 491">[p. 491]</button> the Regulations are made? The Regulations you have been good enough to send me, but what I want to know is the name of the constitutional body or power by whose authority such a law is made.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, Pall Mall, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">100/Candidates/1689.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">War Office, Pall Mall, S.W. <em>18th July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—With reference to your letter of the 8th instant, and previous correspondence, I am directed by the Marquis of Lansdowne to acquaint you that the conditions for admission to the commissioned ranks of the Army are laid down by regulations made by the Secretary of State for War, under the authority of her Majesty the Queen, as signified by Article I. of the Royal Warrant for the Pay, Appointment, Promotion, and Non-effective Pay of the Army.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am to add that this exhausts all the information I am able to afford you on the subject of your enquiry.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Coleridge Grove</span>, M.S.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">D. Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Wandsworth, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Wandsworth, S.W. <em>19th July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I am much obliged for the information you have been good enough to send me in your letter (100/Candidates 1689) of 18th inst., viz., “that the conditions for admission to the commissioned ranks of the Army are laid down by Regulations made by the Secretary of State for War under the authority of her Majesty the Queen, as signified by <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-512" data-page="512" data-folio="492" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n511_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 492">[p. 492]</button> Article I of the Royal Warrant for the Pay, Appointment, Promotion and Non-effective Pay of the Army.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">May I request you to inform me where I can get a copy of this “Royal Warrant,” or to furnish me with a copy?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, Pall Mall, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">100/Candidates/1692.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">War Office, Pall Mall, S.W. <em>23rd July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I am directed by the Secretary of State for War to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 19th instant, and to acquaint you in reply that a copy of the Royal Warrant can be obtained from Messrs. Eyre and Spottiswoode, East Harding Street, Fleet Street, E.C.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Coleridge Grove</span>, M.S.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">D. Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Wandsworth, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>7th August</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I thank you for your letter of 23rd ult. (100/Candidates/1692), and I have obtained a copy of the Royal Warrant from Messrs. Eyre and Spottiswoode.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In your letter of 10th June last you were good enough to acquaint me “that candidates for commissions in the British Army must be of pure European descent, and are also required to be British-born or naturalised British subjects.” In your letter of 18th July last (100/Candidates/1689) you inform me “that the conditions for admission to the commissioned ranks of the Army are laid down by regulations made by the Secretary of State for War under the authority her Majesty the Queen as signified by Article I. of the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-513" data-page="513" data-folio="493" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n512_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 493">[p. 493]</button> Royal Warrant for the Pay, Appointment, Promotion, and Non-effective Pay of the Army.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I need not say how very much obliged I feel to the Secretary of State for War for all your replies, and I now beg further indulgence and favour of his lordship to give me some further explanation on the matter that I need.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In “Article I.—First Appointments,” I do not find a word to exclude British subjects like the Indian-British subjects. The candidates are required to be “persons duly qualified under regulations approved by our Secretary of State.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now I cannot suppose that any such regulations can be made constitutionally under the Warrant by the Secretary of State as would supersede any Act of Parliament or any Proclamations of her Majesty the Queen; but that such regulations can only be made in accordance with Acts of Parliament and Proclamations of the Sovereign. I desire to know whether I am right.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Under this Section I. of the Warrant there is in clause 1<span class="sc">A</span>: “To a duly qualified candidate from a university.” In the regulations for such candidates certain British universities are specified. There are Indian-British subjects who have graduated and are graduating almost every year in some of these universities. There is not a word to exclude such graduates; this would show that the Warrant did not mean to exclude Indians. Under clause 3 there is: “By open competition.” Here again no exclusion is made by the Warrant of British-Indian subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">And it stands to reason that it could not be otherwise. The Act of Parliament of 1833 enacted “that no Native of the said territory (meaning India), nor any natural born subject of his Majesty resident therein, shall, by reason only of his religion, place of birth, descent, or any of them, be disabled from holding any place, office, or employment under the said Company.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now all the powers, duties, and responsibilities of the Company are transferred to the Queen by another Act of Parliament of 1858, and the entire exclusion of the considerations of religion, place of birth and descent, has remained as binding now as it was by the Act of 1833 for any place, office, or employment under her Majesty. Not only did Parliament not repeal or amend the clause of the Act of 1833, but in far more emphatic and explicit terms the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-514" data-page="514" data-folio="494" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n513_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 494">[p. 494]</button> Sovereign issued a Proclamation, strongly and explicitly confirming, and in the most solemn manner pledging before God and man, with an invocation of the blessing of God, placing her Indian subjects on exactly the same footing as all her Majesty’s other subjects, in these clear words :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“We hold ourselves bound to the Natives of our Indian territory by the same obligations of duty which bind us to all our other subjects ; and these obligations, by the blessing of Almighty God, we shall faithfully and conscientiously fulfil.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“And it is our further will that, so far as may be, our subjects of whatever race or creed, be freely and impartially admitted to offices in our service, the duties of which they may be qualified by their education, ability, and integrity duly to discharge.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“In their prosperity will be our strength, in their contentment our security, and in their gratitude our best reward. And may the God of all power grant to us and to those in authority under us strength to carry out these our wishes for the good of our people.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Nothing can be clearer than that British Indian subjects are most solemnly and honourably pledged to be exactly like all other British subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In 1887 on the occasion of the Great Jubilee, the Queen and Empress of India again confirmed her Proclamation of 1858 in these clear words :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“It had always been, and will always be her earnest desire to maintain unswervingly the principles laid down in the Proclamation published on her assumption of the direct control of the government of India.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I do not see, therefore, how it is possible that the Queen would intend in this Warrant anything contrary not only to Acts of Parliament but to her own most gracious and explicit Proclamations of 1858 and 1887. That our gracious Sovereign and the British people, whose voice and desire she represents, could have been anything but sincere in her Proclamations cannot be admitted for a moment, and it is impossible to believe that her Majesty’s Warrant could have had the least intention of stultifying and superseding Acts of Parliament and falsifying her Majesty’s own great Proclamations, so seriously made to the world on two great and historical occasions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There is this further indication. I find that in the spirit <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-515" data-page="515" data-folio="495" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n514_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 495">[p. 495]</button> of and in accordance with the Acts of 1833 and 1858 and the Proclamations of 1858 and 1887—all the Civil Services of the United Kingdom in every department—Civil, Military, and Naval—are open to the British Indian subjects. There are no doubt some flaws in the rules and their execution, which I cannot refer to in this letter; but the fact is there, that all the Civil Services of the United Kingdom are open to the Indian British subjects to the same extent as to any other British subjects: such as the British people.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There is one other explanation I feel necessary to ask as to the qualifications stated in your letter of June 10—that the Candidates "must be of pure European descent, and are also required to be British-born or naturalised British subjects."</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">This would mean that a Turk or a Russian, or a Bulgarian, or a Spaniard, or any other of European descent can have the qualification of admission by being only naturalised; while natural-<em>born</em> subjects of her Majesty's own British dominions, and even after publicly pledged to be exactly like other British subjects, are to be excluded as only mere helots. Even those born in the Colonies would appear to be thus excluded.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">You will easily see how puzzled I feel at your letter of June 10 last, and I shall feel exceedingly obliged to the Secretary of State for War to give me the necessary explanations.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, Pall Mall, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. 31<em>st August</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I hope you have received my letter of 7th inst., and shall feel obliged to have the explanation I have requested for.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, Pall Mall, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-516"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="516" data-folio="496" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n515_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 496">[p. 496]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">No. 100/Candidates/1709. War Office, London, S.W. <em>22nd September</em>, 1896. <span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I am directed by the Secretary of State for War to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 31st ultimo, and to express his regret that a reply to your former letter, dated 7th August, has unavoidably been delayed. The subject will receive the Marquis of Lansdowne’s consideration on his return to this office, when a further communication will be made to you. I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">G. Lawson.</span> D. Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>10th December</em>, 1896. <span class="sc">Sir</span>,—Referring to your letter No. 100/Candidates/1709. May I request the favour of the Secretary of State for War for a reply to my letter of 7th August last ? I remain, yours faithfully, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span> The Under-Secretary, War Office, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">100/Candidates/1703. War Office, London, S.W. <em>21st December</em>, 1896. <span class="sc">Sir</span>,—With reference to previous correspondence, I am directed by the Secretary of State for War to say that your letter of the 7th August last has received his fullest consideration. I am to acquaint you that in the regulations which govern the admission of candidates to the Army it is clearly laid down that only such candidates as are considered “in all respects suitable to hold a commission in the Army” <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-517" data-page="517" data-folio="497" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n516_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 497">[p. 497]</button> are eligible. It has been decided that pure European descent is an essential qualification.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am further to add that there is nothing whatever, either in the Queen’s Proclamation or in the regulations for the admission of university candidates to which you have referred, which could have the effect of obliging the military authorities to grant commissions in the Army to candidates who are not considered suitable.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have the honour to be, Sir, Your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Arthur Haliburton.</span> D. Naoroji, Esq.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>28th December, 1896.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have received your letter of 21st inst. (100/Candidates/1703).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It appears from your reply that my letter of 7th August last has been misunderstood. Perhaps I have not been clear enough. I briefly recapitulate our correspondence.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I first asked you to furnish me with a copy of the Regulations. You kindly sent me four pamphlets. I read the pamphlets and wrote to you. After quoting the 4th section, under “General Notices” of the paper of the Civil Service Commissioners on the question of the “Nationality” of the candidates, I pointed out that I had not found the qualification of “Nationality” mentioned at all in the pamphlets, and asked whether I was right in understanding that the qualifications given under “General Notices” by the Civil Service Commissioners, applied to the Army examinations, and that they included Indians, as being born in “British dominions” and being thus natural born British subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To this you replied “that candidates for commissions in the British Army must be of pure European descent, and are also required to be British-born or naturalized British subjects.” I thereupon naturally asked you to inform me by what Act of Parliament was this limit laid down for the candidates to the exclusion of other British subjects of her Majesty of other descent and born in her Majesty’s British dominions, such as British India and the Colonies. To this <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-518" data-page="518" data-folio="498" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n517_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 498">[p. 498]</button> you replied that “the conditions for admission to the Army are not laid down by Act of Parliament but by Regulation, and that the Regulations are to the effect already conveyed to you.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I then asked : “I have always understood that the only constitutional authority or power for laying down all such conditions is Parliament, and you say that these conditions are not laid down by Act of Parliament. Then what other constitutional authority has the power and has laid down these conditions, according to which the Regulations are made. What I want to know is the name of the constitutional body or power by whose authority such a law is made.” You replied : “That the conditions of admission to the commissioned ranks of the Army are laid down by Regulations made by the Secretary of State for War, under the authority of her Majesty the Queen, as signified by Article I of the Royal Warrant for the Pay, Appointment, Promotion, and Non-effective Pay of the Army.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I requested, and you kindly informed me, where I could get a copy of the Warrant. I obtained it and then wrote my letter of 7th August last, to which your letter under acknowledgment is the reply. I pointed out in my letter that “In Article I, First Appointments, I do not find a word to exclude British subjects like the Indian British subjects. The candidates are required to be persons duly qualified under Regulations approved by the Secretary of State.” In thus pointing out that the Royal Warrant had not in any way authorised to make any regulations to exclude British Indian subjects, I further said : “Now I cannot suppose that any such regulations can be made constitutionally under the Warrant by the Secretary of State as would supersede any Act of Parliament, or any Proclamations of her Majesty the Queen, but that such regulations can only be made in accordance with Acts of Parliament and Proclamations of the Sovereign. I desire to know whether I am right.” To this the Secretary of State for War has not been pleased to give any reply in your present letter : I beg to ask it again.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For further confirmation of my view, that the Royal Warrant upon which the Regulations are said to be based does not in any way authorise the exclusion of Indian subjects from becoming candidates for commissions in the Army, I cited two points from the Warrant itself : (1) “About <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-519" data-page="519" data-folio="499" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n518_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 499">[p. 499]</button> the eligibility as candidates of graduates of some of the universities, in which no exclusion is made for British Indian graduates of those universities”; and (2) of “Open competition.” I shall deal with point (1) further on when I shall deal with your present letter. On point (2) of “Open competition” laid down in the Queen’s Warrant itself, the Secretary of State has not been pleased to give any explanation. I beg for it again. After expressing my views that the Warrant gave no authority to exclude Indians, I endeavoured to show that it stood to reason that the Queen’s Warrant could not and would not make any such exclusion. I cited the solemn pledges and actions of both her Majesty and Parliament, and said that “it was impossible to believe that her Majesty’s Warrant could have had the least intention of stultifying and superseding Acts of Parliament and falsifying her Majesty’s own great Proclamations so seriously made to the world on two great and historical occasions.” To this there is no reply, and I beg again the Secretary of State’s attention to this part of my letter of 7th August last, and to explain how and by whom could such Acts and Proclamations be superseded and disregarded.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In your letter under reply, you say : “It has been decided that pure European descent is an essential qualification.” But you do not say <em>who</em> has so decided. Parliament has not so decided, her Majesty has not so decided. Who is this mysterious great potentate, superior to the Queen, and superior to Parliament, who had the authority to decide contrary to the express desire and decisions of the Queen, the Parliament, and the British people, represented by them ?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">You further say, “that in the Regulations which govern the admission of candidates to the Army, it is clearly laid down that only such candidates as are considered in all respects suitable to hold a commission in the Army are eligible.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">First of all, the Regulations have no authority to exclude the Indians as I have already explained. Next, even according to your extract, it does not at all follow that the British Indians are excluded. If any of them offer to show themselves and can show themselves “in all respects suitable to hold a commission” by submitting themselves to all the tests and conditions which are required from candidates, they are also eligible. What, then, had anyone the right to exclude <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-520" data-page="520" data-folio="500" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n519_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 500">[p. 500]</button> Indians altogether, contrary to the authoritative decisions of her Majesty and Parliament? Can any man in his senses believe that out of 225,000,000 of British Indians you will not get a few thousands who are “in all respects suitable to hold a commission in the Army” if fair and honest trial is allowed to them as candidates? Parliament does not believe so— her Majesty does not believe so.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Referring to the second paragraph of your present letter, I never said anything of “obliging the military authorities to grant a commission in the Army to anyone not considered suitable.” My question is not for the “unsuitable” graduates, but for those who offer to prove themselves to be “suitable” under the <em>same</em> conditions as are required from any other candidates. The Royal Warrant does not exclude any section of the graduates of the specified universities. Unauthorised Regulations are of no force.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">My last enquiry in my letter of 7th August last has also not been replied to. How is it that a Turk, or a Russian, or any of European descent can by mere naturalization become a candidate; and a British Indian subject, born in her Majesty’s own dominions, and to whom equality is pledged in every honourable and binding manner should be excluded?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I fully trust that the Secretary of State would be good enough to reply to all my enquiries.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The only authoritative rational explanations I have met with are :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><ul class="editorial-list"><li>1. Lord Salisbury’s direct and significant words, “India must be bled,” and that “all talk of pledges, equality, etc., was political hypocrisy.”</li><li>2. Lord Lytton has explicitly said with regard to the actions of the authorities that they were “so many deliberate and transparent subterfuges for stultifying the Act and reducing it to a dead letter.” He further says, “I do not hesitate to say that both the Governments of England and India appear to me up to the present moment unable to answer satisfactorily the charge of having taken every means in their power of breaking to the heart the words of promise they had uttered to the ear.”</li><li>3. A committee of five Members of the Council of the India Office declared as far back as 1860 that the British were exposed to the charge of “keeping promise to the ear and breaking it to the hope.”</li></ul></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-521"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="521" data-folio="501" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n520_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 501">[p. 501]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I respectfully ask whether the action of the War Office making arbitrary and unauthorised Regulations to exclude the British Indians from the commissions in the Army falls under the above explanations or whether there is any other satisfactory explanation.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I once more sincerely trust that the Secretary of State will be pleased to reply to all my questions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">100/Candidates/1745. War Office, London, S.W. <em>25th January</em>, 1897.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir,</span>—I am directed to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 28th ultimo, and to express the regret of the Secretary of State for War that my letter of the 21st idem did not impart to you the full information it was intended to convey.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In reference to the doubts which you apparently entertain as to the authority under which the Regulations for admission to the Army are made, I am to call your attention to the preamble of the “Royal Warrant for Pay, Promotion, etc.,” from which you will learn that such Warrant has the express sanction of her Majesty the Queen, and that the Secretary of State for War is appointed by her Majesty to be “the sole administrator and interpreter” thereof, and “empowered to issue such detailed instructions in reference thereto as he may from time to time deem necessary.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The detailed instructions governing the grant of commissions in the Army are made by the Secretary of State under the above authority, and are, as you are already aware, to be found in paragraph 1 of the Regulations for admission to the Royal Military College, Sandhurst, and to the Royal Military Academy, Woolwich, when read in conjunction with Appendix I. to those Regulations.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, D. Naoroji, Esq. <span class="sc">Arthur Haliburton.</span></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-522"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="522" data-folio="502" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n521_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 502">[p. 502]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>26th January</em>, 1897.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have received your letter of 25th inst. (No. 100/ Candidates/1745).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am sorry to trouble the Secretary of State again because my enquiry remains just as much unanswered as before. In my letter of 7th August last my very first question is this :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“Now I cannot suppose that any such regulations can be made constitutionally under the Warrant by the Secretary of State as would supersede any Act of Parliament or any Proclamation of her Majesty the Queen; but that such regulations can only be made in accordance with Acts of Parliament and Proclamations of the Sovereign. I desire to know whether I am right.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To this question I have yet received no reply. If I am right, then the Secretary of State has no right or powers to exercise such absolute power as he claims in your present letter. No executive officer has any despotic powers to make any regulations which can at all contravene any Act of Parliament or Proclamation of the Queen sanctioned by Parliament. I, therefore, submit that the Secretary of State has no power or authority whatever to exclude British-Indian subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Secretary of State by claiming such powers as your letter assumes, exposes her Majesty and Parliament to the charge of un-English hypocrisy, <em>i.e.</em>, that her Majesty on the one hand proclaims to the world repeatedly that British-Indian subjects are exactly like the British subjects, and on the other hand stultifies and breaks her own pledges by giving to the Secretary of State authority to disregard her Proclamations. The same charge will apply to Parliament for allowing such a thing. I anticipated this in my letter of 7th August last, when I said, after citing the Acts and Proclamations :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“That our Gracious Sovereign and the British people, whose voice and desires she represents, could have been anything but sincere in her Proclamations cannot be admitted for a moment, and it is impossible to believe that her Majesty's Warrant could have had the least intention of stultifying and superseding Acts of Parliament and falsifying <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-523" data-page="523" data-folio="503" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n522_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 503">[p. 503]</button> her Majesty’s own great Proclamations, so seriously made before the world on two great and historical occasions.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The fact is that the Warrant gives no such power or authority to make any regulations contrary to Acts or Proclamations. Every power given to any executive officer is subordinate to and restricted by Acts of Parliament and Proclamations of the Queen, unless the Secretary of State means to attribute to her Majesty and Parliament mean political hypocrisy. I therefore ask again my very first question in my letter of 7th August last, which I have quoted above, and in this letter I restrict myself to that question.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>12th May</em>, 1897.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I shall feel much obliged by being favoured with the reply to my letter of 26th January, 1897.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">100/Candidates/1786.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">War Office, London, S.W. <em>25th May</em>, 1897.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—With reference to your letter of the 12th inst., requesting that a reply may be sent to your letter of the 26th January last, I am directed by the Secretary of State for War to acquaint you that he has nothing to add to the various communications which have been already made to you relative to candidates for commissions in the British Army.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Arthur Haliburton.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-524"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="524" data-folio="504" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n523_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 504">[p. 504]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Washington House, 72, Anerley Park, S.E. <em>16th January, 1900.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—Referring to your letter of 10th June, 1896, in which you inform me that “candidates for commissions in the British Army must be of pure European descent, and are also required to be British born or naturalized British subjects,” I have to request you to kindly inform me whether any alteration has been made in the rule above cited; if so, kindly inform what it is.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Yours obediently, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span> The Under-Secretary of State, War Office, Pall Mall, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">No. 100/Candidates/2097. War Office, London, S.W. <em>23rd January, 1900.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I am directed by the Secretary of State for War to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 16th inst., and to acquaint you in reply that no change has been made in the regulations which require that candidates for commissions in the British Army must be of pure European descent, and must also be British born or naturalized British subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have the honour to be, etc., etc., <span class="sc">G. Fleetwood Wilson.</span> Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Washington House, 72, Anerley Park, S.E.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-525"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="525" data-folio="505" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n524_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 505">[p. 505]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><em>Copies of Correspondence between the Admiralty and Mr. Dadabhai Naoroji.</em></h2><h3 class="sec-h2">IX.</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W. <em>5th June</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I find in the paper for “Examinations held under the direction of the Civil Service Commissioners,” which the Secretary has been good enough to supply me, that I have to apply to you for a copy of the Regulations for “Examinations for the Navy.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">May I request you to supply me with a copy of these Regulations?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, yours faithfully, Secretary, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji</span>. Admiralty, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">N. Admiralty, <em>8th June</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have received and laid before My Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty your letter of the 5th instant, and in forwarding a copy of the Regulations for entering the Royal Navy as an assistant clerk, etc., I am to acquaint you that the power of nominating candidates is vested in the hands of the First Lord of the Admiralty, to whose private secretary all applications for nominations should be addressed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The regulations for the entry of surgeons and engineers are to follow.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Evan Macgregor</span>. Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-526"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="526" data-folio="506" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n525_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 506">[p. 506]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W. <em>14th June, 1896.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir,</span>—I am much obliged for your letter of 8th inst. (N.), enclosing papers for examinations in connexion with the Navy.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the paper of Civil Service Commissioners to which I referred in my last letter I find under the heading “General Notices,” among the qualifications of candidates, Section 4, as follows:—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>“4.—<span class="sc">Nationality</span>: A person born in a foreign country who can prove that his father or his paternal grandfather was born in British Dominions is, if he has not expatriated himself under the Naturalization Act of 1870, admissible as a natural-born British subject to all open competitions; which he is in other respects qualified to enter, except those for Student Interpreterships.”</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I do not find this qualification of “nationality” mentioned in the papers you have been good enough to send me. You will oblige me much by informing me whether I am right in understanding that the qualifications given under “General Notices” by the Civil Service Commissioners apply to the examinations for the Navy, and that therefore Indians of British India are included as being born in “British Dominions,” and being thus “natural-born British subjects.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Secretary, Admiralty, London, S.W.</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">N.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Admiralty, <em>6th July, 1896.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir,</span>—In reply to your letter of the 14th ultimo, relative to the qualifications as regards nationality of candidates for entry in the Royal Navy, I am commanded by my Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty to acquaint you that appointments in the Civil Service, which are under the Regulations of the Civil Service Commissioners, are subject to the terms of the paragraph respecting nationality in the “General Notices” of the Civil Service Commissioners, to which you <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-527" data-page="527" data-folio="507" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n526_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 507">[p. 507]</button> refer in your letter, but appointments in the Royal Navy are not governed by these Regulations.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">R. D. Awdry.</span> Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Wandsworth, S.W. <em>9th July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I beg to acknowledge with thanks the receipt of your letter of 6th inst. (N.) informing me “that appointments in the Civil Service, which are under the Regulations of the Civil Service Commissioners, are subject to the terms of the paragraph respecting Nationality in the ‘General Notices’ of the Civil Service Commissioners to which you refer in your letter, but appointments in the Royal Navy are not governed by these Regulations.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have therefore to request you to oblige me further by informing me by what Regulations these appointments to the Royal Navy are governed, and according to what Act of Parliament are any regulations laid down with regard to the nationality of the candidates for the Royal Navy.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span> The Secretary, Admiralty, Whitehall, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">N.                                                 Admiralty, <em>16th July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—In reply to your letter of the 9th instant.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am commanded by my Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty to acquaint you that appointments to the Royal Navy are not governed by Act of Parliament, but by Regulations laid down by the Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty in virtue of the powers conferred on them by Patent.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Evan Macgregor.</span> Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Wandsworth, S.W.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-528"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="528" data-folio="508" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n527_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 508">[p. 508]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Wandsworth, S.W. <em>17th July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have received your letter of the 16th inst. (N.) and thank you for informing me that appointments to the Royal Navy are governed by Regulations laid down by the Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty in virtue of the Powers conferred on them by Patent.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">May I request you to inform me where I can get a copy of this “Patent” or to furnish me with a copy ? I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji</span>. The Secretary, Admiralty, Whitehall, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">N.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Admiralty, <em>25th July</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—In reply to your letter of the 17th instant, I am commanded by my Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty to acquaint you that the Patent of the Board of Admiralty will be found in the report of the Royal Commission upon the Administration of the Army and the Navy, Parliamentary Paper C-5979 of 1890.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Evan Macgregor</span>. Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Wandsworth, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>8th August</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I thank you for your letter of 25th ult. (N). I have obtained copy of Parliamentary Paper C-5979 of 1890.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In my letter of 14th June last I requested you to inform me “whether I am right in understanding that the qualifications given under ‘General Notices’ by the Civil Service Commissioners apply to the Examinations for the Navy, and that therefore Indians of British India are included, as <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-529" data-page="529" data-folio="509" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n528_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 509">[p. 509]</button> being born in ‘British dominions’ and being thus ‘natural-born British subjects.’”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To this you did not favour me with a direct reply, but in your reply of (N) 6th ult., you informed me that “appointments in the Royal Navy are not governed by these Regulations.” In your letter (N.) of 16th ult. you informed me “that appointments to the Royal Navy are not governed by Act of Parliament, but by Regulations laid down by the Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty in virtue of the powers conferred on them by Patent.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I may here offer my sincere thanks for all the replies you have sent me, to the Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty; and I now beg further indulgence and favour to give me some further explanations on the matter that I need.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have seen the Patent in the Return above mentioned, and I find nothing therein to exclude the British Indian subjects from the cadetships in the Navy.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">And it stands to reason that it could not be otherwise. I cannot suppose that under the British Constitutional Government any Patent would be issued, or any Regulation would be made by any Department, in supersession or invalidation of any Act of Parliament, or any public Proclamations of the Queen; and the Patent very properly does not seem to do anything of the kind.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Act of Parliament of 1833 enacted that no Native of the said territory (meaning India), nor any natural-born subject of his Majesty resident therein, shall, by reason only of his religion, place of birth or descent, or any of them, be disabled from holding any place, office or employment under the said Company.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now, all the powers, duties and responsibilities of the Company are transferred to the Queen by another Act of Parliament of 1858, and the entire exclusion of the considerations of religion, place of birth or descent, has remained as binding now as it was by the Act of 1833, for any place, office or employment under her Majesty. Not only did Parliament not repeal or amend the clause of the Act of 1833, but in far more emphatic and explicit terms the Sovereign issued a Proclamation, strongly and explicitly confirming, and in the most solemn manner pledging before God and man, with an invocation of the blessing of God, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-530" data-page="530" data-folio="510" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n529_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 510">[p. 510]</button> placing her Indian subjects on exactly the same footing as all her Majesty’s other subjects, in these clear words :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ We hold ourselves bound to the Natives of our Indian territory by the same obligations of duty which bind us to all our other subjects ; and these obligations by the blessing of Almighty God we shall faithfully and conscientiously fulfil.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ And it is our further will that so far as may be, our subjects, of whatever race or creed, be freely and impartially admitted to offices in our service the duties of which they may be qualified by their education, ability and integrity duly to discharge.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ In their prosperity will be our strength, in their contentment our security, and in their gratitude our best reward. And may the God of all power grant to us, and to those in authority under us, strength to carry out these our wishes for the good of our people.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Nothing can be clearer than that British-Indian subjects are most solemnly and honourably pledged to be exactly like all other British subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In 1887, on the occasion of the Great Jubilee, the Queen and Empress of India again confirmed her Proclamation of 1858 in these clear words :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“ It had always been, and will always be, her earnest desire to maintain unswervingly the principles laid down in the Proclamation published on her assumption of the direct control of the government of India.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I do not see, therefore, how it is possible that the Queen would intend in this Patent anything contrary not only to Acts of Parliament but to her own most gracious and explicit Proclamations of 1858 and 1887. That our gracious Sovereign and the British people, whose will and desire she represents, could have been anything but sincere in her Proclamations cannot be admitted for a moment, and it is impossible to believe that her Majesty’s Patent could have had the least intention of stultifying and superseding Acts of Parliament and falsifying her Majesty’s own great Proclamations so seriously made to the world on two great and historical occasions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">There is this further indication. I find that in the spirit of and in accordance with the Acts of 1833 and 1858 and the Proclamations of 1858 and 1887 all the Civil Services of the United Kingdom in every department—civil, military, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-531" data-page="531" data-folio="511" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n530_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 511">[p. 511]</button> naval—are open to the British-Indian subjects. There are, no doubt, some flaws in the rules and their execution which I cannot refer to in this letter ; but the fact is there, that all the Civil Services of the United Kingdom are open to the British-Indian subjects to the same extent as to any other British subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">You yourself have been good enough to point out to me in your letter (N., 6th July last) “that appointments in the Civil Service, which are under the regulations of the Civil Service Commissioners, are subject to the terms of the paragraph respecting nationality in the ‘General Notices’ of the Civil Service Commissioners to which you refer in your letter.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I shall, therefore, be much obliged by informing me that the cadetships in the Navy are as open to British-Indian subjects as they are to all other British subjects of her Majesty. If not I shall be thankful for an explanation for the exclusion of British-Indian subjects, contrary to Acts of Parliament, confirmed and proclaimed by two great Proclamations of her Majesty the Queen.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Secretary, Admiralty, Whitehall, London, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">N. Admiralty, S.W. 10<em>th August</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have laid before My Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty your letter of the 8th inst. respecting Naval cadetships for British-Indian subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, your obedient Servant, <span class="sc">Evan Macgregor.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Dadabhai Naoroji, Esq., Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Admiralty, Whitehall. 13<em>th August</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have received and read your letter of the 8th inst. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-532" data-page="532" data-folio="512" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n531_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 512">[p. 512]</button> I must demur to your arguments and conclusions; but after all the discussion between us is only academical, as no application for a cadetship for a Native of India has been made to me during my tenure of office, nor is any one now before me.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">If there were I should, in accordance with the practice of my predecessors, and with the unlimited right which I possess to exercise an unfettered choice, select such boys among the whole of the applicants as seemed most likely to me from their antecedents, their surroundings, their physical attributes and other considerations, to become the most efficient officers, with the greatest prospect of being successful in leading and governing British seamen.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">That is the responsibility which rests upon me, and which I should do my best to discharge.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, yours very faithfully, <span class="sc">George J. Goschen.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. 16<em>th August</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I feel exceedingly thankful for your reply of 13th inst. As I do not know the reasons of your demur to my arguments and conclusions, I cannot say anything about it.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is not a mere academical matter. It is of the most vital importance to the Indians, and I may say to England also. Honest fulfilment, or non-fulfilment of the pledges of the Acts and Proclamations, makes all the difference between Indians' British Citizenship and British Slavery, between prosperity and "bleeding" poverty, between the blessing and the curse of British Rule, between honour and dishonour of the British name, between the loyalty and disloyalty of the Indians, and between the stability and instability of the British Indian Empire which in reality is mainly the British Empire.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">But your kind and straight letter makes it unnecessary for me to say anything more upon this matter. Your letter shows, if I understand it rightly, that the Indian-British subjects are not to be excluded from the Navy on account of their nationality, race or creed, but that their applications <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-533" data-page="533" data-folio="513" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n532_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 513">[p. 513]</button> will be fairly and honestly treated for fitness as those of any British-born subjects.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">That no application has been hitherto made is owing to the firm belief among the Indians that they are excluded on account of their nationality, not only from all the higher ranks but even from the lower ranks of seamen, stoker, etc. One venturesome Parsee (Mr. Pirozshaw Dorabji) however, notwithstanding this belief, prepared himself at Hull, to make himself fit for a sea life and for a stokership—and applied to the Admiralty for employment as stoker. I have not the papers before me and I write from memory. But you will be able to verify by seeing the correspondence that has taken place. I think no objection was taken as to his fitness, but the final reply was, I think, that Europeans were preferred, or something to that effect.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Further the Admiralty asks from India a contribution of £100,000 per year, but it does not seem to see, that if India is a partner in the Empire and not a slave, the Indians ought in all fairness to have a return in the employment of the Indians to the extent of the amount of their share. This incident further confirms the belief that the Admiralty does not mean to treat India fairly as a partner and as entitled to a fair share in the Imperial Services in proportion to their part. This incident, I mention simply in illustration. It is a large and very important subject, and I do not think it would be appropriate for me to intrude it in this correspondence.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As the misapprehension of exclusion is removed by your kind letter, I would request your further consideration with regard to one point in connexion therewith. Your assurance needs some clear statement as to what qualifications will be considered necessary for fitness. You will, I have no doubt, at once see this need. The Indians are unfairly and heavily handicapped under present arrangements, by not being able to apply on spot for first appointments, as the people of the United Kingdom are; and before the Indians come over all the distance from India to England, under several difficulties, they must be able to see whether they possess the necessary qualifications and can calculate upon fair chances of success.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">For instance, with regard to the different Civil Services of both the United Kingdom and India, the necessary qualifications are laid down; and even handicapped as the Indians are <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-534" data-page="534" data-folio="514" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n533_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 514">[p. 514]</button> with the necessity of coming over here, they know what to expect; just the same for Law, Medicine, Universities, Colleges, Engineering or other professions.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They will abide by whatever the standards and kinds of qualifications there may be for any employment. All that is needed is that they should know before leaving India what will be required of them for admission.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, yours very faithfully, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span> Rt. Hon. G. J. Goschen, First Lord of the Admiralty.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Admiralty, Whitehall, <em>24th August, 1896.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Sir,—In reply to your letter of the 16th inst., I feel bound to say that I think you have failed to appreciate the drift of my letter, or to draw the natural inferences from it.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I called the discussion of the general arguments from Proclamations, etc., “academical” as regards the point of the admission of Indian-born boys as cadets into the Naval Service, because no one has a right to a nomination, the selection of candidates resting entirely with the First Lord of the Admiralty.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">A discussion therefore as to <em>rights</em> becomes “academical.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I further explained how my choice would be guided by my wish to secure officers who would be best qualified to govern British seamen. I must frankly say that I was under the impression that the words I used would lead you to infer that preference would be given to those of British parentage.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I cannot for one moment admit that the, to my mind, very small contribution of India to the cost of the Navy, viz., £100,000 or so, towards a total expenditure of some £22,000,000, establishes any kind of claim to admission to the ranks of the Navy. It is a contribution in respect of protection given, and nothing else.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Generally speaking, the Regulations of the Navy in respect to the parentage of those who are to be admitted to the various ranks approximate to those of the Army and have been in force for many years.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, yours very faithfully, <span class="sc">George J. Goschen.</span></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-535"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="535" data-folio="515" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n534_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 515">[p. 515]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>28th August, 1896.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have received your letter of 24th inst.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I shall not at present say anything about the academical discussion. Nor is the present correspondence fit to discuss the important question of the exaction of £100,000 from India without any voice of the Indians. Small or large amount is not material. Whether India is partner or slave in the Empire—that is the question. But that discussion must rest at present. I had mentioned this simply as an illustrative incident about the belief in India that the British Indians were absolutely excluded, on account of their nationality, no matter however qualified they may be. And after all your present letter shows that the belief was well founded.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In your first letter of 13th inst. you directed my attention to the fact that no application had been made by an Indian. Then you pointed out (if such an application were made) that you would follow the practice of your predecessors, and with the unlimited right which you possessed to exercise an unfettered choice, you would select such boys among <em>the whole</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-13-fn-007" href="#sec-13-fn-007" data-fn-target="sec-13-fn-007" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> of the applicants as seemed most likely to you from their antecedents, their surroundings, their physical attributes and other considerations to secure the most efficient officers with the greatest prospect of being successful in leading and governing British seamen. You did not say what the practice of your predecessors was. You said only what you would do. You stated the qualifications about the <em>whole</em> of the applicants, but not a word about entire disqualification of Indians on account of their nationality alone, though this reply was to my questions, which were distinctly directed, as follows, to that particular point of “nationality.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">First.—In my letter of 14th June last, I asked :—</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">“I do not find this qualification of ‘Nationality’ mentioned in the papers you have been good enough to send me. You will oblige me much by informing me whether I am right in understanding that the qualifications given under ‘General Notices’ by the Civil Service Commissioners apply to the examinations for the Navy, and that therefore Indians of British India are included as being born in British <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-536" data-page="536" data-folio="516" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n535_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 516">[p. 516]</button> dominions and being thus natural-born British subjects.” The qualification especially referred to by me was clearly mentioned to be that of “ Nationality.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">To this my first enquiry you did not favour me with a direct reply.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Second.—In my letter of 8th inst. I again as pointedly made my enquiry as follows : “ I shall therefore be much obliged by informing me that the cadetships in the Navy are as open to British-Indian subjects as they are to all other British subjects of her Majesty.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In your reply of 13th inst. to this enquiry, there was not a word said that Indians were positively excluded as Indians. On the contrary, as I have stated above, you laid all stress upon qualifications for fitness. If Indians were disqualified by their “ Nationality,” the reply of 13th inst. had no meaning—as laying stress upon qualifications of fitness, unlimited right of selection, unfettered choice, etc., etc.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Your present letter of 24th inst. is again as puzzling. You say that “ no one has a right to a ‘ nomination.’ ” I did not ask or claim any such right, any more than any other British subject had.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">You say “ the selection of candidates rests entirely with the First Lord of the Admiralty.” But really this must be certainly on some definite principles, and founded upon and ordered by some constitutional authority (which has not been pointed out in your letter), and not on the mere absolute whim or the despotic will of the First Lord, as if he were an Oriental despot. But what is still stranger is, that if an Indian is excluded because he is an Indian, and if the First Lord has positively determined not to consider any Indian application, what was the good of telling the Indian that he would consider the <em>whole</em> of the applicants. You say : “ I further explained how my choice would be guided by my wish to secure officers who would be best qualified to govern British seamen.” This shows that it was certain qualifications you wanted in each individual applicant, and not a decided exclusion of an Indian if he possessed the qualifications. But if you left yourself no choice, and would give no consideration to an Indian applicant, what was the good of telling him about how your choice was to be guided ?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">You say : “ I frankly say that I was under the impression that the words I used would lead you to infer that preference <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-537" data-page="537" data-folio="517" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n536_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 517">[p. 517]</button> would be given to those of British parentage.” “Preference” would mean that if there were an Indian and a British applicant of equal merits “preference” would be given to the British. But even of the “preference” of British parentage there was no clear indication in your letter of 13th inst. But even supposing that such an inference was possible, then if the Indian was wholly and absolutely excluded as an Indian “preference” is only an idle word and means nothing.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Thus you will see that the matter is still left vague in your present letter of 24th inst.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I again put the question directly.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Is the Indian to be excluded on the ground of his nationality or not? It is desirable that such a vital matter to above two hundreds of millions of British subjects should not rest on mere misleading and vague “drifts,” “inferences,” or “impressions,” but must be clear in statement.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In the last sentence of your letter, where there is something like a clear statement, though under cover, the matter is still left vague. You say: “Generally speaking the Regulations of the Navy in respect of the parentage of those who are to be admitted to the various ranks approximate to those of the Army and have been in force for many years.” A wrong does not become a right by being enforced.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">However, you have not favoured me as to what these Regulations of the Army are, and your words “<em>generally</em> speaking” and “approximate” still leave a certain vagueness as if the Regulations of the Navy and Army were not quite alike.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have a reply from the War Office. That reply, however unconstitutional and unauthorised, un-British and dishonourable to solemn pledges it appears to me, is at least direct and clear and not left to mere impressions or inferences. This reply is “that candidates for commissions in the British Army must be of pure European descent, and are also required to be British-born or naturalized British subjects.”</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now I request you kindly to inform me whether in the Navy also there is exactly, as above, the same absolute and complete exclusion of British Indians as in the Army, so that the matter may be dealt with in its true character. If the exclusion in the Navy is exactly like that in the Army then all that is said about “preference,” “qualification,” “exercise of unlimited right of selection,” “fitness,” “no applications had been received from Indians,” and leaving matters to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-538" data-page="538" data-folio="518" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n537_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 518">[p. 518]</button> “drifts,” “impressions,” and “inferences” would appear to be mere excuse and of no good or use.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Kindly make the matter clear, whatever it may be.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am informed that there were or are some Japanese in the Navy. I do not know whether this is true. If it is so I would be obliged to be informed what their position was or is.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, yours very faithfully, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Rt. Hon. G. J. Goschen, First Lord of the Admiralty, Whitehall, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>5th December, 1896.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have addressed a letter to you on the 28th August last.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I shall feel obliged for reply to it.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I remain, yours faithfully, <span class="sc">Dadabhai Naoroji.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Rt. Hon. G. J. Goschen, First Lord of the Admiralty, Whitehall, S.W.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Admiralty, Whitehall. <em>8th December</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—In reply to your letter of the 28th August allow me to say in all courtesy that you seem to me to be endeavouring to create a grievance—while none, to my knowledge, has existed hitherto—by your efforts to draw from me an unnecessary declaration.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I have nothing to add to my previous letters—which appear to me to be perfectly intelligible to anyone who wishes to understand them—beyond pointing out to you, in reply to one of your questions, that the regulations which govern admission to various branches in the Navy and Army are accessible to the public, and will furnish you with the means of testing the statements I made to you with regard to them. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-539" data-page="539" data-folio="519" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/povertyunbritish00naoruoft/page/n538_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 519">[p. 519]</button> I regret that I cannot undertake to continue this correspondence.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I am, Sir, yours very faithfully, <span class="sc">George J. Goschen.</span></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cambridge Lodge, West Hill Road, Southfields, S.W. <em>10th December</em>, 1896.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Sir</span>,—I have received your letter of the 8th inst., and I am very sorry I am forced to trouble you again. I am not going into any argument upon your letter. But there seems to me some misunderstanding, and I shall feel much obliged by its being cleared up by you. The question is simply this, and to which the War Office has given a direct reply : Is the Navy open to British-Indian subjects, as it is open to the inhabitants of the United Kingdom ; or is it not—the regulations being the same for all ?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The reason for a reply is twofold. The Indians then will know whether they should apply or not at all.
